INTRODUCTION. 


This volume commences with the S#a¢-chakra-niripaza 
of Purmananda Svami which forms the sixth chapter of the 
hitherto unpublished work of that author entitled Shritattva- 
chintamazi. The latter is a voluminous treatise in Twenty-five 
Chapters. The MSS. in the library of the Asiatic Society 
of Bengal covers some 400 pages of close writing on paper 
larger than foolscap 
The first Chapter deals with Tattvabodha ; the Second with 
the necessity of and different modes of Diks#a; the Third 
Chapter gives directions regarding the selection of the place of 
Diks2a,. Kurmmachakra, Vastuyaga and Vighnanashaka 
Mandala ( danger destroying circle ) : also as to the appropriate 
sacrifices and Mantras of the Devatas to be worshipped in ~ 
the Mandala: the Fourth Chapter is devoted to the rules 
relating to the erection of the Mazdapa ; the Fifth Chapter 
speaks of the auxiliary Devatas and their places in Diksha 
and the rules relating to their Puja; of the kumbha (jar) ; of 
the metals to be used in its making and its size and shape 

The sixth chapter of it here published contains a description — 
of the six centres, the so-called Miuladhara, Svadiskihana 
Mazipura, Anahata, Vishuddha, and Ajna Chakras or Lotuses 
(padma) a knowledge of which is essential for the understanding © 
of the Yooa method dealt with in the Tantras. To this work 
there isa commentary, apparently written by Kali-charaza. — 
Though there is no direct statement of such authorship yet the _ 
commentaries on the S#a¢-chakra and the Paduka-panchaka are 
written in the same style, and under v. 43 of the Shat-chakra 
the Commentator says that he has made clear the matter 
there referred to in the commentary (which was written by 
Kalicharaza) on the Paduka panchaka-stotra (paduka panchaka 
stotre spashii-kritam) Kalicharaza begins his commentary on . 


il 


the Padukapanchaka by saying that it is composed by himself 
(Kalicharama prakashayet). To the commentary of Kali- 
charana are added notes by the Commentator Shangkara, who 
appears to have used a text slightly different from that of 
Kalicharana. The variant readings have been here noted 
This work and its commentaries are followed by the 
Paduka-panchaka with the commentary of Kalicharaza already 
referred to. The latter is a careful commentator, who has dealt 
with his subject in a thorough manner but the difficulties of 
both the text and his commentary are such that the reader 
will be glad to avail himself of the notes from the third 
commentary here referred to, by Vishvanatha on the second 
_ Patala of the Kaivalya-kalika Tantra, which, (though so far as 
Iam aware not now available) dealt, in this portion of it with 
‘the same subject and much on the same lines as Purminanda’s 
_Shaf-chakra. Rather, it should be said that, as the Kaivalya- 
kalika is held to have been an original Tantra, it is not unlikely 
that Pirvzananda was indebted to it for his own work. In 
some matters, Vishvanatha has explained things in his own 
way, simply and in a manner which goes straight to the point 
The Sfat-chakra contains fifty fives verses. To these is 
added another, here numbered 537 () and located at the end 
of the volume which though not noticed, by either Kali-charana 
or Shangkara, is given by Baladeva’s text | 
The work may be conveniently divided into eight sections. — 
namely, (r) verses I to 3 which deal with the Nadis, (2) verses 
4 to 73 treating of the Muladhara Padma (3) verses I4 (078, 


which deal with the SvadisZz¢fana (4) verses I9 tO टा with ie 
... Manipiira (5) verses 22 to 27 with Anihata (6) verses 28 to 
. 3t with Vishuddha, (7) verses 32 to 38 with Ajna, (8) verses 39 
to 49 with the Sahasrdra. and (9) verses 50 to 54 with the 
ee passage of Kundalini from the Mi adhara to Sahasrara plercing 


_ on her way thither the six chakras—a Tantrika Yooa process _ 
_ Known as Shat-chakra-bheda or as some call it Bhitashuddhi. | 


ili 


It is not necessary to deal further with the subject matter 
here, as [ have, in preparation for publication, an English trans- 
lation of the text now given. The Séaé-chakra-nirupaza and 
the Paduka-panchaka with notes of Kalicharaza were first 
published in Shakabda, I778 (A. D. I858) by the Sucharu 
Press, Calcutta, under the editorship of the late Anandachandra 

Vedantavagisha. This edition which is rare having been long 
out of print, has been used in the preparation of this volume. 
In 7276 (A.D. i869) another edition of the Shat-chakra with 
the commentary of Ramavallabha was published by Kalidas 
Bhattacharyya, along with the Paduka-panchaka and its com- 
mentary by Kali-charana. Kaliprasanna Vidyaratna in I294 
(A.D. 887) published the Sai-chakra together with a number 
of other books on Yoga. This edition of the Séa¢-chakra 
contained the commentary of Rama-vallabha though it is not 
" acknowledged as such by the editor. There have been several 
other cheap publications on Yoga which contain the Séa‘- 
chakra niriipaza. Some are provided with Bengali translations 
but none with notes. The commentaries of Vishvanatha and 
the notes from Shangkara are published for the first time. —
